The overlay pauses capture before opening. When paused, the buffer and recorder repeat the last frame instead of advancing. This is why the overlay freezes during recording or replay.

The freeze is caused by the intentional pause. Pausing capture prevents the overlay menu from appearing in the recording, but it also forces the buffer to repeat the last frame. On Windows, GDI GetDC(None) captures the screen including any visible overlay window, which explains why the pause was added.

Several fixes for the overlay freeze are under consideration. Pausing capture only briefly during screenshots is one option. Another is to hide the overlay from the capture entirely, or to capture the game window while excluding the overlay window. Skipping frames instead of repeating the last frame would prevent frozen video, though it would make the recording shorter.

The best UX is to keep capturing the game while the overlay is open, rather than freezing on a single frame. A transparent fullscreen overlay would still be captured by GDI. Hiding the overlay during grabs causes flicker. Capturing only the game window instead of the full screen, or using Windows Graphics Capture with window exclusion, would avoid both the freeze and the flicker.

Windows 10 2004 and later supports `SetWindowDisplayAffinity(WDA_EXCLUDEFROMCAPTURE)`. This hides the overlay from screen capture while keeping it visible to the user. It would eliminate the freeze and prevent the overlay UI from appearing in recordings. Linux has no equivalent.

Pinned overlay boxes should remain visible even after the overlay is closed. Currently hiding the fullscreen transparent window removes all boxes. The design needs either a transparent overlay that stays up but ignores clicks, or separate always-on-top windows for each pinned widget.

A single webview cannot selectively ignore clicks on empty areas while keeping the pinned boxes interactive. The overlay window must stay fullscreen, transparent, and always-on-top to support this behavior.

The overlay window should hide when closed and unpinned to avoid consuming resources. When pinned widgets are active, the window must stay visible but allow clicks on empty areas while keeping the pinned boxes interactive. Tauri 2's `set_ignore_cursor_events` makes the entire window ignore clicks, which would break the pinned boxes. Per-region click-through is not easily supported.

A fullscreen window that stays visible while pinned would still block all clicks and interfere with the game. The overlay must be click-through on empty areas so the game remains interactive. On Windows, this can be done by setting `WS_EX_TRANSPARENT` or using `SetWindowLong` to ignore cursor events by default, then removing that behavior when the mouse moves over a pinned box.

A timer will poll cursor position to toggle click-through on the overlay window. Clicks pass through empty areas by default, but the window becomes interactive when the mouse moves over a pinned box. Linux can use X11 input shapes or GTK regions for similar behavior, though it is more fragile.

**Replay length.** The 30, 45, and 60 buttons only choose how much is saved. They do not reopen the panel. The number on Last is the length that will be written. In Settings you can add others, such as 90 or 120, and those show up in the overlay too. Switching among lengths that already fit does not throw away the frames already kept.

**Recording.** The grab keeps running while the panel is open. On Windows the panel is left out of the capture, so it should not appear in the picture and the game should not sit on one frame. A screenshot on Linux still steps aside for that one picture, then comes back.

**Boxes.** Drag a title to move a box. Pin keeps it on screen after the panel closes. Alt+X opens the full panel again. Closing Orbit still closes the overlay.

**Audio.** The mixer is named Audio. It shows the game, the speakers, then one other source. More opens the rest.

**Playing.** A song, or a browser video such as YouTube, shows its cover when the app provides one, with pause, previous, next, and that app's volume. It is the cover the system already has, not a second copy of the video.

**Gallery.** Shots, recordings, and replays show a preview. A recording that cannot be read falls back to an icon.

**Screenshot sound.** It is a short chime in the same tones as the library, not a camera click. Settings can turn it off or change its volume, and Preview plays it.

**Does the overlay affect in-game performance?** The replay buffer starts with the game when replay is on, whether the panel is open or not. That is the capture cost, and it was already there. The overlay window is not created until the first time you open it, unless something is pinned, in which case only those boxes come up at startup. While it is open it is a fullscreen transparent window on top, so the compositor does a little extra work. Audio and the player only ask the system every couple of seconds while that view is actually showing. A closed panel is hidden, is not paused into the recording, and is not left sitting in the picture.

A logic issue exists: the thread sleeps for one second before calling take(), which drains.

The first drain primes the accumulator and returns None. The second drain captures roughly one second of frames. If duplication fails to open, frames stays None and the previous FPS is preserved. If the duplication process dies, take() reopens the counter automatically.

The `drain` method returns `None` for both primed and failed states, so the code cannot tell them apart. This causes unnecessary reopens on every first drain. A `Pull` enum with `Waiting`, `Count(u32)`, and `Dead` variants would cleanly separate these cases.

The sampling thread sleeps one second between samples. The first drain is immediate, followed by the sleep and then the count.

A static screen may cause the prime acquire to time out without consuming a frame. The next drain could then include frames from before sampling started, producing one bad sample. If prime times out, the next second's acquire accumulates all frames since duplication began, which may exceed one second.

I am refining the FPS calculation. Dividing frames by elapsed time will fix the systematic over-count that makes 60fps videos read as 61 or 62. I will avoid rounding to common rates so real spikes are not hidden.

I will compute FPS over a one-second rolling window instead of the raw sample interval. This makes 60fps video display more stably and avoids rounding spikes.

I realized the rolling window FPS calculation is tricky. Each sampled frame count represents frames since the previous drain, so the first sample's timestamp actually predates its own count. Measuring elapsed time from that first sample would undercount the true interval. I need to adjust the window boundaries to avoid double-counting or misaligning the time span.

I will use a simple exponential moving average to smooth the FPS display. The raw rate is computed as frames divided by elapsed time, then blended with the previous smoothed value using a 65/35 weight. This prevents one-off spikes from jumping the reading while still allowing genuine rate changes to settle over a few samples.

The EMA weight is being set to 0.5/0.5 so one extra frame does not stick as 62. The drain loop will run until the timeout expires to avoid missing frames that would inflate the next interval. The max(1) guard is the key bug because it adds phantom frames; removing it fixes the systematic bias.

The issue involves `std::path::Path::new(&binary)` in the Rust code.

The compiler sees a move in `unwrap_or(binary)` before the borrow from `Path::new(&binary)`. Rust evaluates arguments before the receiver, so `binary` is moved into the fallback before it can be borrowed.

The borrow-after-move error likely stems from the `.unwrap_or(binary)` call, not the `&binary` borrow. The compiler appears to treat the fallback argument as moving `binary` before it can be borrowed.

The fix is to use `unwrap_or_else(|| binary.clone())` so the fallback is cloned instead of moved. Another option is to chain `and_then(|stem| stem.to_str())` and pass `binary.as_str()` directly to `unwrap_or`.

The `windows_list_inner` path uses `unwrap_or_default`, so it avoids the move issue entirely. Only the pactl and Linux paths are affected. The bug went undetected because checks run on Linux, where the code compiles but fails at runtime.

Fixing a borrow issue. The Windows release build is still in progress, but it might succeed if the Windows path compiles despite this Linux-only bug.

`parse_pactl` compiles on all platforms including Windows without cfg-gating.

The borrow of moved value error in audio.rs:238 persists.

The real bug has been identified.

The borrow-after-move error persists. When `name` is empty, `binary` is moved into `name`, but the code still tries to borrow `binary` for the `exe` path.

The fix is to clone `binary` before moving it into `name`, or compute the `exe` path before the move.

The fix is to compute the `exe` path before moving `binary` into `name`. This avoids the borrow-after-move error entirely.
